Chapter 01 — Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 209. Mummaiyaal Ulaganda Sarukkam. Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar Puranam. Following the Thirunaalai Povaar Nayanar Puranam in the Mummaiyaal Ulaganda Sarukkam, the Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar Puranam is placed. First, we will reflect on the brief history of the Nayanar, and then we can delve into the detailed Puranam of the Nayanar. The country where Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar was born is Thondai Nadu. This land is renowned for its greatness, eminence, and antiquity. It is the land that attained the blessing of Uma Devi performing penance to embrace her Lord. Thondai Nadu is a place where great families, excelling in righteous conduct and impartiality, flourish. Sekkizhar Peruman has extensively sung about Thondai Nadu, the birthplace of Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar, in the Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar Puranam. Sekkizhar Peruman sings about the various landscapes within Thondai Nadu, namely Kurinji, Mullai, Marudham, Neidhal, and Paalai, and also about the Shiva temples located in these regions that are glorified in Thevaram hymns. He then describes the mixing of these landscapes (thinai mayakkam). Following this, he extols the greatness of Kanchipuram, the city where the Nayanar was born. He also elaborates on the history of Ammaiyaar (Goddess Parashakti) performing penance there, specifically detailing the story of Kamakshi Ammai, the all-powerful Parashakti, coming here to perform penance and the Kachi Thirukkamak Kottam. Subsequently, he describes the prosperity of the city. After singing about all these aspects, Sekkizhar Peruman, having extensively narrated the Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar Puranam, then begins the Nayanar's story. Our Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar was born in Kanchipuram, in the Ekaaliyar (washerman) community. He possessed a loving heart, adherence to righteous principles, and a hereditary devotion to Shiva. From the day he was born, the Nayanar dedicated his mind, speech, and body to the sacred feet of Lord Shiva. Due to his nature of understanding and honoring the intentions of Shiva's devotees, the Nayanar came to be known as Thiruk Kurippu Tondar. The Nayanar lived in the great city of Kanchi, born into the washerman community, washing clothes for the people, and was excellent in his truthfulness. He had a heart that would melt at the mere mention of Lord Shiva's name. With great eagerness, he would wash the clothes of devotees whose hearts melted at the mere mention of Lord Shiva's name. He would remove the impurities from the clothes of Shiva's devotees, just as he was removing the impurities of his own three-fold birth (mummalap piravi). One day, Lord Shiva, intending to bestow grace upon the Nayanar, decided to test his sincere devotion. In a very cold season, Lord Shiva appeared before Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar in the guise of a Shiva devotee, wearing a very old, dirty, and tattered cloth. Upon seeing the Shiva devotee, the Nayanar was overjoyed and welcomed him with reverence. Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar, understanding the intention of the visiting Shiva devotee, asked, "Oh Lord, your body seems weary. Please grant me this tattered cloth you are wearing so I may wash it." He humbly requested, "If you give me the garment you are wearing, I will clean and wash it for you." The Shiva devotee who had come replied, "This tattered cloth is so dirty that it can no longer be worn. Moreover, the cold is very severe now.

Therefore, if I give you this garment, I will not be able to withstand the cold, so I will not give it to you." He then added, "If you can wash and dry this cloth and return it by this evening, then take it and quickly wash it." Hearing this, Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar said, "Swami, as you have commanded, I will return it before evening." Hearing this, the Shiva devotee who had come said, "If you do not quickly take it, wash it, dry it, and bring it back by evening, you will be harming this body," and he handed the tattered cloth to Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar. Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar took the garment to wash it properly in the water, intending to return it at the appointed time. As he was washing and drying it, the Lord had a divine intention. At that moment, by the grace of the Lord, rain began to fall incessantly. The rain poured heavily without even a moment's pause. Therefore, the Nayanar was unable to dry it. But he had promised the ascetic that he would wash and dry this garment and return it by this evening. The rain was falling without stopping. "What shall I do?" he wondered. He waited for a while, thinking that the incessant rain might stop, but the rain continued to fall without ceasing. Therefore, Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar was deeply distressed, thinking, "My service to the devotee has been ruined! The rain is falling incessantly! Even if I were to take the washed cloth home to dry it in the shade, the rain is not allowing even that!" He was deeply saddened. Not only that, but he also thought, "The body of that great ascetic, soaked in this rain, will become very cold, and that cold will harm his body, causing him suffering. I have become the cause of his suffering!" He was filled with great sorrow. Therefore, he resolved to strike his head against the washing stone, lamenting, "I have caused harm to the body of Lord Shiva's devotee, making his body shiver!" He prepared to strike his head against the stone where clothes were washed. Just as his head was about to strike the rock, Lord Shiva's bangle-adorned hand, the lotus-like red hand of Ekambara Nayagar, who resides in Kanchi, reached out and held the Nayanar's head. It held the Nayanar's head so that it could not strike the rock. The rain that had been falling until then ceased, and a shower of flowers fell. Ekambaranathar, along with his consort Kamakshi Ammaiyar, appeared on his Rishabha (bull) vehicle and bestowed his grace upon the Nayanar. Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar stood with folded hands, worshipping the Lord. Then, the Lord addressed Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar, saying, "We have made known your steadfast devotion to all three worlds. You shall now live in our eternal Shiva Loka, never to be separated from us." With these words of grace, he vanished into Thiruekambaram. This is the brief history of Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar. Now, according to our tradition, we shall continue to reflect extensively on the sacred history of Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar, following the Thondathogai (summary work), Thiruthondar Thiru Andhadhi (type work), and Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam (expanded work). Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 02 — Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 210. Mummaiyaal Ulaganda Sarukkam. Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar Puranam. In the Periya Puranam, also known as Thiruthondar Puranam, the Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar Puranam is placed after the Thirunaalai Povaar Nayanar Puranam. This section describes the life history and character of the Nayanar named Thiruk Kurippu Tondar. In the Mummaiyaal Ulaganda Sarukkam, following the Puranams of Murthi Nayanar, Muruga Nayanar, Rudra Pasupathi Nayanar, and Thirunaalai Povaar Nayanar, the Puranam of Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar is the fifth in sequence. Sekkizhar Peruman has composed this Puranam with 128 verses. First, we can begin to reflect extensively on the Nayanar's history according to the order of the summary work, then the type work, and then Sekkizhar Peruman's expanded work. Firstly, in the Thiruthondathogai composed by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, Swamigal has sung, "Thiruk Kurippu Tondar tam adiyaarkkum adiyen" (I am a servant to the servants of Thiruk Kurippu Tondar). The meaning of this is that "I am a servant to the devotees of Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar." The Nayanar came to be known by this name because he understood the intentions of Lord Shiva's devotees and served them according to their wishes. For this reason, it is also understood that Lord Shiva came to him with a dirty garment, and the Nayanar, understanding the devotee's intention, requested and received the garment to wash and clean it for him. Now, in the type work, Thiruthondar Thiru Andhadhi, Nambi Andar Nambi composes the following verse about the Nayanar: "Mandhum punal sadaiyaan thamar thoo chetri vaattum vagai vindu mazhai mugil veedaadhu ozhiyin yaan veevan ennaa mindum padar paarai muttum ezhilaar Thiruk Kurippu Tondar kulam Kachi Ekaaliyar thangal thol kulame" (If the clouds, which are like the water-laden matted locks of Shiva, do not cease their tormenting rain, I shall die, said the beautiful Thiruk Kurippu Tondar, who would strike the spreading rock; his lineage is the ancient lineage of the Ekaaliyars of Kanchi). Now, let us reflect on the sacred history of the Nayanar through Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, the expanded work. The tradition that Sekkizhar Peruman adopted in the Nayanar's histories is to describe the country and town they lived in, the rivers of that country, its prosperity, the prosperity of the town, the temples located there, the Lord enshrined there, and also the Nayanar's lineage, family, and tradition, and then to sing about the Nayanar's sacred service. That is the tradition Sekkizhar Peruman adopted. He has composed this Puranam according to that tradition. The country where the Nayanar was born is known as Thondai Nadu. This Thondai Nadu is a country that has performed a greater penance than any other country. Therefore, it is a country of immense glory. What was that penance? The penance performed by that good Thondai Nadu was the great penance where Uma Devi, the mother who bestows boundless compassion on all beings, performed severe penance towards Lord Shiva, standing in the path of the Agamas, praised by the four Vedas, to embrace her Lord. This is the good Thondai Nadu. In Kanchipuram, located in this Thondai Nadu, our Mother Parashakti, with the names Elavaar Kuzhali and Kamakshi, performed penance towards the Lord. She performed worship according to the Agamic tradition. Therefore, Thondai Nadu possesses such greatness. Hence, it is called Thondai Nannadu (good Thondai Nadu). Thus, Sekkizhar Peruman introduces Thondai Nadu in the first verse. The verse is as follows: "Eyum maaru pal uyirkalukku ellaiyil karunai Thaayaan thaniyaayina thalaivarai thazhuva Aayu naanmarai potra nindru arunthavam puriya Thooya maathavan seythathu Thondai nannadu." This is that sacred verse. Sekkizhar Peruman begins to sing, "Eyum maaru pal uyirkalum" (as it suits all beings).

The meaning of this is that the compassion of our Goddess, though boundless, is bestowed upon each being in different ways, according to their karma and maturity. Parashakti, the one who bestows compassion on all beings according to their karma, is therefore the Mother of Compassion. While other mothers in the world give bodies to souls and nurture them, that body becomes the root cause of the soul's ailments, leading to further physical burdens. However, Parashakti, the mother of all, gives bodies to beings to remove the impurities of their ailments, nurtures them through their karmas, removes those karmas, and ensures that no further physical burdens befall them. Therefore, she becomes the unique leader who bestows compassion on all beings as it suits them. Thus, she becomes the unique mother of compassion. Such Uma Devi, Parashakti, performed penance here for her unique Lord. "Thani" means unique or singular. Furthermore, since Parashakti Ammaiyar came to Kanchipuram, separated from the Lord to perform penance, the meaning that the Lord became "thani" (alone) also applies here. Thus, Thondai Nadu is the country where Ammaiyar performed penance to embrace her unique Lord, Lord Shiva, who was separated from her. The city where Ammaiyar performed penance is the great city of Kanchi. Therefore, this country performed great penance. When Ambika requested to perform worship according to the Agamic tradition, the Lord revealed that Kanchi was the suitable place for it. Thondai Nadu has earned the merit of having Kanchipuram within it. If a country has earned merit, it means that great Shiva dharma flourished there, bringing well-being and prosperity to its inhabitants. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman has described the penance performed by Thondai Nannadu, categorizing it differently from Kurinji to Neidhal, and presented it in this Puranam. The Goddess, who performed such penance, performs the penance of Shiva Puja here. Sekkizhar Peruman sings about it as "Aayum naanmarai potra nindru" (standing praised by the discerning four Vedas). "Aaythal" means to discern or clarify. The Vedas, having discerned the characteristics of the Lord, held Lord Shiva as the supreme reality in their hearts. Thus, the Mother, who bestows compassion on all beings, performs Shiva Puja here to show beings the path to worship and attain that supreme reality held within the hearts of the Vedas. The Vedas stand praising this. The penance and worship performed by Parashakti Ammaiyar to embrace the Lord are extensively explained in this history from verse 1135 to verse 1142. Thondai Nadu possesses such greatness, penance, and merit. Sekkizhar Peruman praises it as Thondai Nannadu. "Nannadu" (good country) implies that the goodness lies in the people living there without deviating from righteous conduct. Sekkizhar Peruman elaborates on this in the subsequent verses. Now, in the next five verses, he sings about the greatness of Thondai Nadu, the Nayanar's country. Then, from verse 1083 to 1126, he describes the nature and prosperity of Thondai Nadu. By doing so, he explains that Thondai Nadu encompasses all four land regions and mixed land regions, and that all of them bestow the great life of Shiva's merit, as explained by our teacher Sekkizhar Peruman. Beyond that, he describes the glory and prosperity of Kanchipuram, the Nayanar's city, from 1127 to 1189, as a description of the city. After that, from 1190 to 1207, he narrates the history of Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar. This is the structure of this Puranam. Therefore, although this Puranam, the Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar Puranam, consists of 128 verses, Sekkizhar Peruman has described the glories of Thondai Nadu, the Shiva temples located there, the greatness of Kanchipuram, the penance performed by Ammaiyar, and other historical events in 110 verses, and then narrated the sacred history of the Nayanar in the subsequent 17 verses. In Periya Puranam, it is Sekkizhar Peruman's tradition to describe the glories, prosperity, and reasons for Shiva's merit of a country in great detail and depth when he first sings about it. Therefore, this Nayanar Puranam is the first place in Periya Puranam where Thondai Nadu is mentioned. Hence, he describes Thondai Nadu in such detail here. The description of the country and city is more extensive than the length of Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar's history. Sekkizhar Peruman was born in Thondai Nadu. Kunnathur, where Sekkizhar Peruman was born, is located in Thondai Nadu. Therefore, some say that Sekkizhar Peruman sang in this manner due to his love for his own country. While that is partly true, during Sekkizhar Peruman's time, Thondai Nadu was the country of the Cholas who commissioned the Thiruthondar Puranam. Kanchipuram flourished as a Chola city. Moreover, if we compare the descriptions of Chola Nadu and Pandya Nadu in Periya Puranam, the extent of description given to Thondai Nadu here is relatively small. Then why did he describe Thondai Nadu so extensively in this history? The ultimate purpose of Periya Puranam, the Thiruthondar Puranam, is the sacred form of Lord Shiva's devotees and the Shiva Puja they performed. This is the place where Ammaiyar, the ruler of the world, demonstrates Shiva Puja according to Agamic rules. Therefore, it was essential for Sekkizhar Peruman to describe the greatness of Thondai Nadu in great detail. Thus, to illustrate the Shiva Puja performed by Ambika, the descriptions of the country's glory and prosperity, meaning the glories of Thondai Nadu, were all related to Lord Shiva, and therefore, it became necessary to sing about them extensively. Therefore, the prosperity of the four land regions of Kurinji, Mullai, Marudham, and Neidhal, which are parts of Thondai Nadu where Ammaiyar performed penance, and the prosperity of the land regions where these parts are mixed, and the necessity to categorize them according to Tamil grammatical rules, and how all these regions are associated with Lord Shiva, and how the prosperity of this country is all related to Lord Shiva, and how it was a country rich in Shiva's merit and Shiva dharma – to explain all these, Sekkizhar Peruman sang in this manner. The nature of this epic, Periya Puranam, is to exemplify the rules of Tamil literature by singing about the five landscapes (thinai) of Kurinji, Mullai, Marudham, and Neidhal, and their intermingling (thinai mayakkam). Sekkizhar Peruman has composed it in this Puranam as an example of that rule. Because, while other countries sung in Periya Puranam have parts of these four landscapes, Thondai Nadu is the country that possesses all five thinai regions and their mixed thinai regions. Therefore, Thondai Nadu is worthy of being an example of Tamil literary rules. For these reasons, too, we must understand that Sekkizhar Peruman has sung so extensively. Moreover, how a country, city, and people should be, and why a country, city, and people should be respected, is exemplified here and should be contemplated. Furthermore, in the Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar Puranam, Sekkizhar Peruman's skill in presenting the essence of Agamas to inspire Shiva Puja in the first section with five-lined Kalinilai Thurai, and then the section describing the worship of the Ashtamurthi with eight qualities using eight-lined Viruthams, and the history of removing the impurities of birth by following the four-footed path of Chariyaadi with four-lined Kochaga Kalippa, should also be noted. We will continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 03 — Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 211. Mummaiyaal Ulaganda Sarukkam. Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar Puranam. Thondai Nadu is a land of boundless and ancient glories. It is a country with many prosperous cities possessing strong, great walls. The prosperity of a country is not about new wealth that comes and goes, but rather the true prosperity that has endured from ancient times without diminishing. Many such prosperous cities are located in this country. In this Thondai Nadu, citizens of excellent conduct and noble character reside. "Excellent conduct and noble character" refers to truthfulness and the quality of never intentionally harming others. The citizens of Thondai Nadu live without deviating from impartial conduct. What is impartial conduct? It is to act without considering "this person is mine" or "this person is a stranger," or "this is good" or "this is bad," but rather to adhere to righteousness, standing impartially like the beam of a balance scale. To illustrate that the citizens of Thondai Nadu live without deviating from impartiality, Sekkizhar Peruman cites an ancient story prevalent in Thondai Nadu and incorporates it into his verses. The story of Pazhaiyanur Neeli is known to everyone. Specifically, the story of Pazhaiyanur Neeli has been passed down through generations among the people of Thondai Nadu, especially in the northern districts of Tamil Nadu. In the story of Pazhaiyanur Neeli, there is an account of how the Vellalars acted impartially and upheld justice. The Vellalars of Pazhaiyanur, who desired overt goodness, were courageous enough to sacrifice their lives for justice when blame fell upon them, and indeed they did. Sekkizhar Peruman sings that Thondai Nadu is a country of such Vellalars who sacrificed their lives to uphold their word. This story of Pazhaiyanur Neeli is still prevalent among the people of the northern districts of Tamil Nadu as an example of how the Vellalars kept their word. The story of Pazhaiyanur Neeli is as follows: A long time ago, a merchant lived with his wife. However, the merchant had an affair with a courtesan. So, his wife tried to reform her husband. At that time, the merchant, her husband, took his wife aside and killed her. The blame for killing his wife, for killing a woman, became a demon named Neeli. This demon Neeli pursued the merchant to kill him. To escape from this demon Neeli, he went to a sorcerer and asked for a way out. The sorcerer gave the merchant a magical sword. He said, "Wherever you go, carry this magical sword with you. As long as this magical sword is with you, the demon Neeli will not be able to harm you." So, he always kept the magical sword with him. One day, he came to a village called Pazhaiyanur. At that time, the demon Neeli took the form of a woman. She broke a dry stick, made it into the shape of a child, carried it on her hip, and followed the merchant. In Pazhaiyanur, there was an assembly of 70 Vellalar elders. The merchant, knowing that the demon Neeli, in the form of a woman with a baby, was following him, sought refuge before the assembly of 70 Vellalars. He sought refuge, asking them to protect him from that woman. Neeli, the woman, also came before their assembly and cried out, "This is my husband, and this is our child. He has abandoned us, falling for courtesans, and has run away. So, you must reunite us and help us live together." The demon in the form of a woman thus pleaded. But the merchant denied the woman's claim, saying, "This is not a woman, but a demon named Neeli," to the Vellalar elders. At that moment, Neeli let go of the child she had on her hip, and it ran to the merchant, calling "Father." Therefore, the 70 Vellalar elders could not understand what the truth was. So, they said, "We will give our verdict tomorrow." Therefore, the assembly members told them, "Stay here together tonight." But the merchant had the magical sword in his hand, didn't he? So, the demon Neeli, in the form of a woman, said, "He has a sword in his hand. If we stay together, he will cut us. So, take the sword from his hand and make him stay here without the sword." But the merchant said, "If I don't have that sword, the demon will kill me." There was also the child between them. So, the deceived Vellalars took the sword from his hand and asked the merchant to stay with her without the sword. Furthermore, the 70 Vellalars also promised, "If any harm comes to his life, we, all 70 of us, will give our lives in exchange." So, as they requested, Neeli stayed with the merchant that night. At that time, the demon Neeli tore the merchant apart, killed him, and avenged herself, then left. Knowing this, the Vellalars were deeply saddened. Hadn't they promised to give their lives if any harm came to the merchant? Therefore, they now weighed their lives and the promise they had made, considering the promise they had given to be superior to their lives. So, the 70 Vellalars lit a fire, plunged into it, and attained the sacred feet of Lord Shiva at the Thiruvallangadu Shiva temple. Thus, Pazhaiyanur was the village where 70 Vellalars lived, who, without deviating from impartiality, sacrificed their lives for their word. It is located in Thondai Mandalam. Pazhaiyanur is near Thiruvallangadu. This is the story of Pazhaiyanur Neeli. This very ancient and true history has been sung by Thirugnanasambandar Peruman in the Thiruvallangadu Thevaram. Thirugnanasambandar Peruman sang, "Munai natpaai vanchap padutthu orutthi vaazhnaal kollum vagai kettu anjum Pazhaiyanur" (Pazhaiyanur, which fears hearing how a woman, pretending to be a friend, deceitfully took a life). Umapathi Sivachariyar, who sang the history of Sekkizhar Peruman, has narrated this story of Pazhaiyanur Neeli in the Thiruthondar Purana Varalaru, singing about the 70 Vellalars who, without failing in their word, sacrificed their lives by plunging into fire, their sacrifice, and how they attained the sacred feet of Lord Shiva. The pond where the Vellalars plunged into fire is still known as Neeli Kulam (Neeli Pond) there. On the bank of that pond, there is a temple with idols of the 70 Vellalars and Neeli. Both the temple and the pond are located in Pazhaiyanur. Pazhaiyanur is near Thiruvallangadu. The descendants of the Vellalar community still serve as excellent devotees, bearing the sacred feet of Thiruvallangadu Appar. The fragrance of the truthfulness of these Vellalars still permeates this Thondai Nadu. The Vellalars there desired the well-being of the merchant and his wife, wishing them to live together in harmony. Moreover, they always desired good. These 70 Vellalars made themselves devotees of God's dharma and truth, and unitedly sacrificed their lives. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman refers to them as Siruthondar (small devotees) in this verse. When the action they intended for good ended in evil, they considered that the blame for the merchant's loss of life, due to his adherence to their word, fell upon them. That fault came to them unknowingly. Therefore, they weighed their lives, their word, their promise, and their truthfulness, and considering which was superior or which was worth protecting, they chose their word. They had said that if any harm came to the merchant's life, they would give their 70 lives in exchange. They considered that word to be superior to their own lives and protected it without faltering, descending into the pit of fire and sacrificing their lives. This is their special quality. Pazhaiyanur was the village where such great people, who did not deviate from justice, lived. Thondai Nadu is the country that encompasses it. Thus, Sekkizhar Peruman praises the greatness of Thondai Nadu, filled with such noble people. Furthermore, Sekkizhar Peruman sings that the people of Chera Nadu claim kinship with the people of Thondai Nadu, calling them "maithunar" (brother-in-law). Thondai Nadu possesses such greatness. Chera Nadu is the present-day Malai Nadu, Kerala. Sekkizhar Peruman, when singing about Chera Nadu, sings about Cheraman Peruman Nayanar. Cheraman Peruman Nayanar was one who revered the sacred ash, considering it the very form of Lord Shiva's grace. One day, while he was riding an elephant, a washerman came towards him. He was carrying alkaline earth on his head. At that moment, it rained. The alkaline earth on the washerman's head dissolved and spread all over his body, making him appear white. Cheraman Peruman Nayanar, who was riding the elephant, saw the washerman covered in alkaline earth. This reminded him of sacred ash. So, he dismounted from the elephant and worshipped the washerman. The washerman replied, "I am Adiyen Adiyannan (your servant, the washerman)." Hearing this, Cheraman Peruman Nayanar, even after realizing that the white substance on his body was not sacred ash but alkaline earth, still remembered the sacred ash form when he saw the washerman's appearance. So, he said to the washerman, "Adiyen Adichcheran (I am your servant, Cheran). You reminded me of the sacred ash form. Go without worry." He sent him off. The country ruled by such a great Cheraman Peruman was Malai Nadu. Sekkizhar Peruman calls this Malai Nadu "Thirumalai Nadu" here. "Thirumalai" refers to Mount Kailash. By the command of Lord Shiva, the Lord of Thirumalai (Mount Kailash), Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the Lord of Souls, ascended Mount Kailash on a white elephant from this Malai Nadu. Following him, Cheraman also rode a horse and reached Thirumalai. Therefore, this Malai Nadu is connected to Thirumalai, Kailash. Hence, Sekkizhar Peruman also refers to it as Thirumalai Nadu. The warriors of this Chera Nadu maintained a special relationship of kinship with Thondai Nadu. There is also a history for this. Once, a rebellion broke out in Chera Nadu. So, the people of that country scattered in many directions. Among them, a woman from a merchant family sought refuge in a Vellalar's house in Thondai Nannadu. Those Vellalars raised that woman with love, as if she were their own daughter. After the rebellion in Chera Nadu subsided, her relatives came looking for her and wished to take her back. At that time, the Vellalars, who had raised her as their own daughter, said that she was indeed their daughter, and they sent her off with dowry and arranged her marriage. Therefore, it is a tradition for the people of Chera Nadu to celebrate kinship with the people of Thondai Nadu. A verse in Thondai Mandalam Sathagam also explains this: "Koththalar Kodhai viyan Chera mandala kombai thampaal Vaitthirundhu aangu aval than kelir nedi vara avarkke Uyththiruvorkkum varisaiyum maatru udanum sendru Maithunak kenmai padaiththathu andro Thondai Mandalam" (Did not Thondai Mandalam, by keeping the beautiful woman from the great Chera Mandalam with them, and when her relatives came searching, send her to them with honors for both, and thus establish kinship?). There is also another history: Karikala Chola, the king who improved Kanchipuram and ruled in Thondai Mandalam, married women from the Chera lineage for his son and grandson. Because of this relationship, the Chera warriors did not fight against Karikala Chola's warriors but stood by them, defeating other kings and celebrating their kinship. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman sings that Thondai Nadu is a country that celebrates kinship with such a great Chera Nadu. We will continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 04 — Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 212. Mummaiyaal Ulaganda Sarukkam. Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar Puranam. Thondai Nadu is renowned throughout the world for its adherence to the principles of Shiva Dharma. Sekkizhar Peruman now begins to sing about the general characteristics of the four landscapes of this Thondai Nadu. Until now, in the first five verses, Sekkizhar Peruman has praised the nature of Thondai Nadu, highlighting its ancient historical glories. Now, he will describe the fivefold and fourfold land divisions of Thondai Nadu in a general way in five verses. In Tamil tradition, it is customary to divide the lands of this world into five categories (thinai). This has been defined since Tholkappiyam. Tamils divided this world into four land regions. They divided it into five thinai. Kurinji is mountains and mountainous regions, Mullai is forests and forest regions, Marudham is cultivated fields and their surrounding areas, and Neidhal is the sea and coastal regions. Thus, Tamils divided this world into four land regions. The world is characterized by these four divisions. Therefore, the world is referred to as "Naanilam" in Tamil. Besides these, there is a fifth land, Paalai, which arises from the natural transformation of Kurinji and Mullai. Tamils have included this as the fifth, forming five thinai: Kurinji thinai, Mullai thinai, Marudham thinai, Neidhal thinai, and Paalai thinai. Here, Thondai Nadu is characterized by these five types of land regions. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman first describes the fivefold and fourfold land divisions in a general way in five verses. After that, he describes each of the five thinai separately with their specific characteristics. From 1088 to 1091, he describes Kurinji thinai in four verses, from 1092, Paalai thinai, from 1093 to 1096, Mullai thinai in four verses, from 1097 to 1109, Marudham thinai in 13 verses, and from 1110 to 1117, Neidhal thinai in eight verses. After this, he describes the mixing of these thinai (thinai mayakkam). He describes the combination of Neidhal and Kurinji in verse 1118, Marudham and Kurinji in verse 1119, and Mullai and Kurinji in verse 1120. He describes the mixing of Neidhal and Mullai in verse 1121, and Marudham and Mullai in verse 1122. He describes the combination of Neidhal and Marudham in verse 1123. Thus, the section on thinai mayakkam has six types, which is a key point here. Following this, in verse 1124, he generally describes the excellence of the communities in these four land regions. Then, he begins to describe the glory of Kanchipuram, the capital of Thondai Nadu and the city of the Nayanar of this Puranam. As he describes the glory of the country, in the first part, he describes the glory of Kanchipuram related to ancient history, and in the latter part, he describes the city's structure and prosperity. To compose an epic in Tamil, it is a general tradition to sing about these five thinai. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman has composed it in accordance with that tradition. In Thondai Nadu, the Kurinji regions are filled with waterfalls. Precious gems are mixed in those waterfalls. There are Mullai lands where flowers bloom, and bees swarm there. There are Marudham lands where fish leap, breaking the boundaries of the fields. There is the Neidhal land, the sea and coastal region, where fisherwomen wash and dry pearls. In Kurinji, the mountainous region, the Kuravars live. Kurinji is the land. Kuravars are the people of the Kurinji land. Their occupation is cultivating black-eyed peas. They cultivate and harvest them. Among the black-eyed pea plants they cultivate, there are precious gems like sharp stones. So, they pick them up and throw them away. In Thondai Nadu, there is the Mullai land, which is a forest and forest region. There, herds of cows and deer gather and live together. There is the Marudham land, which is a field region. In those fields, birds sit on lotuses and eat food. There is the Neidhal land, the sea and coastal region. There are backwaters (kazhi). Kazhi are places where seawater, pushed by waves, enters and remains on the land. In the Kurinji region of Thondai Nadu, in the mountainous areas, clouds shower pearls from the sky. In the Mullai region, cool clusters of Konrai flowers bloom like golden flowers. In the Marudham land, the cultivated fields, pearls are showered in the silt of the canals. In the Neidhal land, the coastal region, large ships arrive at the ports surrounded by screwpines. Elephants are unloaded from those ships. Clouds are also a source of pearls. Therefore, in the Kurinji region, clouds shower pearls. In the Mullai region, the petals of Konrai trees fall like golden coins. Elephants from foreign countries are brought by ships and landed at the ports. This is what he sang about the Neidhal land. Now he sings about the villages and food of the four land regions. The people living in those respective lands consume food appropriate to the abundant and cultivable produce available there. This is a characteristic feature of Tamil literary grammar. In the Kurinji region, the mountainous area, honey and millet flour are the main foods. In the Mullai land, wild rice and milk are the main foods. In the Marudham land, rice, ghee, sugarcane, and fruits are important food items. In the sea, fish are naturally abundant, so they are their food. The villages in the Kurinji land are called Seeroor. The villages in the Mullai land are called Paadi. In Marudham, they are called Oor. In Neidhal, they are called Pattinam and Paakkam. Now, the appropriate sound for the Kurinji land, the mountainous region, is the Kurinji tune. There, swarms of bees sing Kurinji tunes. In the Mullai land, which is a forest and forest region, drum beats resonate like clouds. In the Marudham land, gentle parrot chicks, chirping in their sweet voices, sit on Marudham trees and play. In the Neidhal lands, there are backwaters where screwpines provide shade and Neidhal flowers bloom. Thus, Sekkizhar Peruman, having generally sung about the four lands, now begins to sing about each thinai individually. First, he sings about the special characteristics of the Kurinji land. Kurinji is mountains and mountainous regions. Here, there are large millet fields cultivated by the Irulars. There are peacocks with their beautiful tails. Another name for the people of Kurinji land is Iravular. Kurinji thinai is the setting for the union of the hero and heroine, which is the primary theme in Akapporul (love poetry). Therefore, Kurinji is sung first. We should recall here the stories of Lord Murugan, the deity of Kurinji, playing his divine games in his own land, seeing his peacocks and Valli Nayaki, the daughter of the Kuravar chieftain, who is like a peacock, in the millet fields. Here, the people and birds, which are among the karupporul (native elements) of Kurinji, are sung about.

Kurinji is a mountain and mountainous region. Therefore, the Kurinji region located in Thondai Nadu, the Kurinji land of Thondai Nadu, has the blessed life of having the sacred mountain of Thirukkalaathi within it. As we saw in the introduction, Sekkizhar Peruman, when he begins to sing about the four lands, sings about how Shiva's grace, Shiva's merit, and Shiva's dharma have flourished in these four lands of Thondai Nadu. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman, who began to sing about the Kurinji land, also sings about the Lord's sacred places located in the Kurinji region of Thondai Nadu. He sings about three such places. Sekkizhar Peruman sings about Thirukkalaathi Malai, Thiruidai Suram, and Thirukkazhukkundram as the sacred places belonging to the Kurinji land in Thondai Nadu. First, Thirukkalaathi. Thondai Nadu is the country that has the sacred mountain of Thirukkalaathi within it. On this Thirukkalaathi mountain in Thondai Nadu, gods come to worship Kalathiappar at noon. Along with them come celestial women. In the Kannappa Nayanar Puranam, when Kannappa Nayanar ascends Thirukkalaathi mountain, he hears the sound of celestial drums at noon. We saw the explanation that at noon, the gods come to worship Kalathiappar. Therefore, here, celestial women also come with the gods to worship Thirukkalaathiappar on the mountaintop. On the same Thirukkalaathi mountain, there are Kodichiyar women belonging to the Kurinji land. But it is in the flowers in the hair of these Kurinji women that bees find great fragrance. Therefore, instead of swarming in the hair of celestial women, bees swarm abundantly in the flowers in the hair of the Kurinji women here. This is because celestial women adorn their hair with celestial flowers like Mandaram and Karpagam, which only have artificial fragrance. But here, the Kodichiyar women, who are the chieftains of the Kurinji land, are women of noble lineage. Therefore, their hair has both natural fragrance and the artificial fragrance of the many flourishing flowers of Kurinji. Not only that, but here, on Thirukkalaathi mountain, the Kurinji land, these Kodichiyar women worship Gnanapoonkothai, also known as Gnanaprasunambikai, who is seated on the left side of Kalathinathar, and have received the fragrance of Ambika's grace. We can recall here the mythological stories about the natural fragrance of Ambika's hair, Gnanaprasunambikai Ammaiyar, and the Lord's sacred verse, "Kongu Ther Vaazhkai." Thus, the bees, first recognizing the superior fragrance of the hair of the Kurinji people, the Kurinji women of Thondai Nadu, found it better to be here than in the hair of celestial women, and leaving the hair of celestial women, they immersed themselves and resided in the hair of the Kodichiyar women. The celestial women witness this. Therefore, they pray to Thirukkalaathiappar, wishing to live as women of the hunter community on this sacred Thirukkalaathi mountain and worship the Lord. They worship Kalathiappar, praying for that opportunity. The celestial women who do not get that opportunity join the Kodichiyar women here and bathe in the mountain springs. Sekkizhar Peruman has composed and sung this in the subsequent verses. We have extensively reflected on the special features, temple myths, and glories of Thirukkalaathi mountain and Thirukkalaathi temple in this Kurinji land in the Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. We can recall that here as well. We will continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 05 — Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 213. Mummaiyaal Ulaganda Sarukkam. Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar Puranam. Sekkizhar Peruman sings about the glories of the Kurinji land located in Thondai Nadu. In the Kurinji region of Thondai Nadu, the sacred place called Thiru Idaichuram is located. Such is the blessedness of the Kurinji region of Thondai Nadu. The celestials and celestial women, considering the greatness of this Thiru Idaichuram located in the Kurinji land of Thondai Nadu, are born here as hunters and huntresses to worship. Such is the greatness of the sacred place called Thiru Idaichuram. This Thiru Idaichuram is the 27th sacred place among the temples sung in Thondai Nadu. It is named Thiru Idaichuram because it is located amidst mountains, between the stony passes (suram) of the mountains. Here, Thirugnanasambandar Peruman, seeing the luminous form of the Lord, sang, "Idaichuram mevya ivar vanam ennae" (What is this forest where he resides in Idaichuram?). This sacred place, Thiru Idaichuram, can be reached by taking the road from Chengalpattu to Thirukkazhukkundram and then turning north towards Thiruppoorur. Thiru Idaichuram is currently known as Thiruvadi Soolam. Here, the Lord's name is Gnanapureeswarar, Idaichuranathar. The Goddess's name is Goparatnambikai, Imaya Madakkodi Ammaiyar. Here, the Sthala Viruksham (sacred tree) is Vilvam, and the Theertham (sacred pond) is Agni Theertham. Ambika, Appar Peruman, Sekkizhar Peruman, Sanathkumarar, and Gautama Rishi have worshipped the Lord here. This village and temple, now known as Thiruvadi Soolam, as sung by Sekkizhar Peruman, are situated in a natural environment surrounded by mountains, adjacent to fields. It is a place where Ambika worshipped in the form of a cow, showering milk. In front of the temple, on the left side, there is a shrine for Varasiddhi Vinayagar. To its right is a pond. Entering the gateway and circumambulating the prakaram (corridor), one finds separate shrines for Brahmandeswarar and Brahmandeswari. The Sthala Viruksham, the Vilvam tree, is located here. Entering the inner gateway, passing through the mandapam, and circumambulating to the right, one finds the Nalvar Perumakkal (the four great devotees). The Vinayagar shrine and the Subramaniar shrine with Valli and Deivanai are next to each other. Next, one can have darshan of Bhairavar. Here, the sanctum sanctorum of the Lord has a moat-like structure. Here, Ambika gives darshan facing south, in a standing posture. The Lord gives darshan facing east. The Moolavar (presiding deity) is a self-manifested Maragatha Lingam (emerald lingam). When deeparadhanai (waving of lamps) is performed for the Shiva Lingam, its light appears very bright. It is a wonderful and beautiful darshan of the Maragatha Lingam with a square pedestal. Thirugnanasambandar Peruman's sacred hymns are dedicated to this Sthalam. Throughout these hymns, the beauty and color of the Lord's Maragatha Lingam are praised. Since Ambika, in the form of a cow, performed abhishekam with milk that bestows wisdom and worshipped Lord Shiva, he is called Gnanapureeswarar, and Ambika is called Govardhanambikai. Here, Ambika is depicted with her left foot slightly forward and her right foot slightly back, in a posture ready to walk towards us. When Lord Shiva set out in the guise of a cowherd to relieve Thirugnanasambandar's fatigue, Ambika also started with him. However, he refused to take Ambika with him. Ambika angrily asked for the reason. He replied, "Thirugnanasambandar is one who drank the milk of wisdom given by you. There cannot be a child in the world who does not know its mother. Any child can easily recognize its mother. I am now going in the disguise of a cowherd. So, if you come with me, Thirugnanasambandar will easily recognize us. Therefore, you stay here." Saying this, the Lord alone left. That is why Ambika remains in the posture of having started, with her foot forward, even today. Here, Gautama and Bhrigu Maharishi have worshipped Idaichuranathar and left. Thirugnanasambandar Peruman was passing this way during his Shiva Sthala Yatra. Having walked a long distance, hunger gnawed at his stomach. As the sun climbed higher, the heat also increased. Tired, Sambandar Peruman sat under a tree to rest. At that time, a cowherd came, wearing a loincloth and carrying a small stick. He had a pot of curd in his hand. Knowing that Sambandar was hungry, he offered him curd to drink. After his fatigue was relieved, the cowherd asked Thirugnanasambandar who he was. Thirugnanasambandar Peruman replied that he was on a Shiva Sthala Yatra. So, the cowherd told Gnanasambandar Peruman that there was a Shiva temple in this forest and asked him to come and sing hymns and have darshan. Sambandar, whose hunger was appeased by the cowherd, listened to his words and had darshan of the Lord here. Sambandar Peruman, seeing that the cowherd, though a cowherd, was very beautiful, followed him. When he reached this temple, the cowherd disappeared, and a bewildered Sambandar prayed to Shiva. Lord Shiva appeared to him and revealed that he had bestowed grace in the form of the cowherd. Sambandar Peruman worshipped Shiva as Idaichuranathar (Lord who left in the middle) and sang hymns, because Shiva had come as a cowherd and left him in the middle. These are the glories of the sacred place called Thiru Idaichuram, or Thiruvadi Soolam. Now, Sekkizhar Peruman sings that the next glory of the Kurinji region of Thondai Nadu is that Thondai Nadu possesses the sacred place called Thirukkazhukkundram. This Thirukkazhukkundram is the 28th sacred place among the temples sung in Thondai Nadu. It is renowned for being sung in Thevaram hymns and Thiruvachagam. It is located very close to Chengalpattu. It is named Thirukkazhukkundram because two eagles worship here in each of the four Yugas. The Vedas themselves are in the form of a mountain here. Therefore, it is also called Vedagiri. Even today, the worship of the eagles can be seen. North Indians call this Pakshi Theertham. Worship takes place underwater once every 12 years. Even in this era, we can clearly see conches forming and floating in the Sangu Theertham at the base of the mountain once every 12 years. Even today, it is a common sight for people to bathe in Sangu Theertham, circumambulate the mountain, have darshan, and be cured of their ailments, as mentioned in the saying "pinithaan thavirum" (ailments will be cured). Some of the special features of Thirukkazhukkundram: Here, the Lord on the mountain is called Vedagireeswarar. The Goddess in the temple on the mountain is Sokkanayagi. In the temple at the base of the mountain, the Lord's name is Bhakthavatsaleswarar. The Goddess's name is Thiripurasundari. Here, the Sthala Maram (sacred tree) is Kadali, which is the banana tree. The Theertham is Sangu Theertham and Pakshi Theertham. Markandeyar, Thirugnanasambandar Peruman, Appar Swamigal, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, Manikkavasagar, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, Sekkizhar Peruman, and others have worshipped here. On this 500-foot high mountain, an eagle comes daily at noon to receive food. We saw that the Vedas themselves are the mountain here. Rig Veda forms the base of this mountain, Yajur Veda the middle part, Sama Veda its foundation, and Atharva Veda the peak of this mountain. Therefore, this mountain is called Vedagiri. The Lord is called Vedagireeswarar. It is a special feature here that there is no Nandi (bull) in front of the Vedagireeswarar sanctum. The Vedagireeswarar temple is a serene place located on the mountain with 565 steps. Once every 12 years, when Guru (Jupiter) enters Kanni Lagna (Virgo ascendant), the Lakshadeepa Peruvizha (festival of a hundred thousand lamps) takes place. Once every 12 years, the beautiful Sangu Theertham, where conches are born, is located here. This miracle is still happening. In the temple located in the middle of the village below, Ambika Thiripurasundari is a self-manifested form. Ambika's idol is made of Ashtagandham, including eight types of fragrant substances. Full abhishekam (sacred bathing) is performed for Ambika only on three days: Aadi Pooram, Panguni Uthiram, and Navami during Navaratri. On other days, worship is performed only at the Goddess's feet. Worshipping Ambika during these three grand abhishekams that occur once a year is believed to remove sins and bring good fortune. Aadi Pooram is celebrated as a 10-day festival. Once, the Vedas went to worship Paramasivan and requested Paramasivan, who resides in Mount Kailash, that they should always praise him. At that time, Lord Shiva told the Vedas, "Go to a place on earth and stand in the form of a mountain with your various parts. If you do so, we will reach you there like a flame." Therefore, the Vedas came to this earthly realm, and at the place where Thirukkazhukkundram is located, the four Vedas stood as a mountain. Rig Veda became the root of that mountain, Yajur Veda the middle, Sama Veda the base, and Atharva Veda the peak. Gold, silver, iron, copper, lead, bronze, and brass, these seven metals, are the Dhatus (elements). These Dhatus, becoming Chandas (metrical forms), are present in that mountain. Kalpam (a unit of time) is its cave. Mimamsa (a school of Hindu philosophy) is the valley of that Vedagiri. Upanishads are the trees of this Veda mountain. Niruttam (etymology) is the waterfalls that flow here. Jyotis (astronomy) is the many gems in the mountain. The principles of Sivagamas are the slopes on the four sides of the Veda mountain. Vyakarana (grammar) is the stones. The 18 Puranas and Agathantras are the forests. Sikshai (phonetics) is the green creepers that are found in this Kshetra. There is a hole in the Vimanam (tower) of Vedagireeswarar. It is believed that Indra comes through it once every 12 years to perform worship in the form of thunder. It is also customary to circumambulate this Veda mountain on Purnima (full moon day). Brahma, Vishnu, Indra, Ashtavasus, Koti Rudras, Nandiyam Peruman, and Ashtakazhugugal (eight eagles) have worshipped Thirumalai Vedagireeswarar. This Sthalam has descriptive names such as Rudrakoti, Brahmapuri, Adinaranayapuram, Nandipuri, and Indrapuri. Around this Thirumalai, there are 14 Theerthams: Indra Theertham, Sambu Theertham, Uttara Theertham, Vasishta Theertham, Sangu Theertham, Meygnana Theertham, Agasthiya Theertham, Markandeya Theertham, Kausika Theertham, Nandi Theertham, Varuna Theertham, Agaligai Theertham, Pakshi Theertham, and Lakshmi Theertham. Here, Sangu Theertham is deep and wide, with an inexhaustible spring even during times of drought. It is a miraculous Theertham where conches are born once every 12 years. This miracle is still occurring today. In this Sthalam, in the first Yuga, two eagles named Chandan and Prachandan, in the second Yuga, two eagle kings named Sampathi and Sadayu, and in the third Yuga, two eagles named Sambukundan and Mahagukundan, were born as eagles due to a curse and attained liberation. Here, festivals such as Lakshadeepa Thiruvizha, Chithirai Thiruvizha, Vidaiyaatri Utsavam, Thiripurasundari Amman Aadi Thiruvizha, Thirukkalyana Thiruvizha, Navaratri Thiruvizha, Karthigai Deepa Thiruvizha, Theppa Thiruvizha, Sangabhishekam, Arudra Darisanam, Sundarar Thiruvizha, Masi Magam, Pavitrotsavam, and Manikkavasagar Thiruvizha are celebrated. We saw that in the temple pond here, conches are born once every 12 years, didn't we? When a conch is born once every 12 years, the waves in the pond become stronger. Foam forms and overflows at the edges of the pond. At that time, the conch washes ashore. Then, the temple priests take the conch on a plate.

At that time, the conch worm inside separates from the conch shell and returns to the water. The priests sit on the steps for this event. Thousands of devotees gather around the pond to witness this rare event. Normally, conches are born in saltwater seas. Here, they appear in ordinary fresh water. Moreover, this happens precisely once every 12 years. Including this conch, seven conches born so far are kept in the temple for devotees' darshan. They say this is a rock-cut temple built by Mahendra Pallavan. There is an extensive Puranam called Thirukkazhukkundram Puranam for this Sthalam. It is the Thirukkazhukkundram Puranam composed by Andagakkavi Veeraraghava Mudaliyar. Thus, the Kurinji region of Thondai Nadu is a country that encompasses the Kurinji land with three sacred places: Thirukkalaathi, Thiru Idaichuram (Thiruvadi Soolam), and Thirukkazhukkundram. We will continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 06 — Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 214, Mummaiyal Ulaganda Sarukkam, Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar Puranam. After singing about the Kurinji region in Thondai Nadu, Sekkizhar Peruman now sings about the Palai region. Palai is a land where Kurinji and Mullai have deviated from their original state. The land suitable for Palai consists of some elevated areas with gravel stones, adjacent to Kurinji and Mullai lands. Its major season is summer, and its minor season is intense midday. Due to its central position, Madhava Sivagnana Yogigal refers to Palai Thinai as the neutral Thinai. Silappathikaram states, "Mullaiyum Kurinjiyum muraimaiyil thirindhu nalliyalbu azhindhu nadungu thuyar uruthu palai enbathor padivam kollum," meaning Mullai and Kurinji, deviating from their natural state, lose their good nature and assume the form of Palai, causing trembling sorrow. Therefore, Palai is not a distinct land; it is a land that arises when Kurinji and Mullai lose their original state and deviate. The deity of this Palai land is Kottravai, also known as Durga. Temples of Durga, who is blue in color and holds a sword in her hand, are located in this Palai region. Now, Sekkizhar Peruman begins to sing about the Mullai region. Mullai is a forest and its surrounding areas. Here, there are many Kurundha trees in the Mullai land. The Mullai creeper spreads over these Kurundha trees, resembling a pandal (canopy). Sekkizhar Peruman refers to it as Poopandal. In Sanskrit, it is called Latha Mandapam. Since Mullai land is a forest-related area, there are forest streams here. When these forest streams overflow with water, they spread across various parts of the Mullai forest. Therefore, their water flow is slow. This water spreads among the delicate Mullai bushes. At that time, soft rabbits hiding there are chased by the water and run hither and thither. Thus, Sekkizhar Peruman sings that among the karupporutkal (elements) of Mullai, Kaniaru is the water body. Kurundham and Mullai are the tree and flower. Rabbit is the animal. The people of Mullai land are called Aichiyars or Idaichiyars. The beauty of the teeth of these Idaichiyars surpasses the beauty of the Mullai buds here. The forehead of the Idaichiyars resembles the crescent moon of the third day. Now, Sekkizhar Peruman sings about the Shiva holy places located in the Mullai region of Thondai Nadu. The first is Thirumullaivayil. The second holy place is Thiruooral, also known as Thakkolam. First, Thirumullaivayil. The eyes of the women in this Mullai region are like swords. Herds of deer, defeated by those eyes, leap and run everywhere. Thirumullaivayil is the holy place worshipped by Thirumal, who has red eyes and is described in Tamil Porul Ilakkanam as the deity of Mullai Thinai, the Mullai land. Here, Lord Shiva resides with pleasure and joy. In Tamil Porul Ilakkanam, each of the four lands has its own deities. For Kurinji, the deity is Lord Muruga. Similarly, for this Mullai land, the deity is Thirumal, also known as Vishnu Murthy. Tholkappiyam states, "Maayon meya kaadurai ulagamum, Seyon meya maivarai ulagamum, Vendhan meya theempunal ulagamum, Varunan meya perumanal ulagamum, Mullai Kurinji Marutha Neythal ena solliya muraiyal sollavum padume," meaning the forest world ruled by Maayon (Thirumal), the mountainous world ruled by Seyon (Muruga), the sweet water world ruled by Vendhan (Indra), and the vast sandy world ruled by Varunan (Varuna) are called Mullai, Kurinji, Marutham, and Neythal in that order. Here, it is stated that Thirumal is the deity of Mullai land among the Mullai karupporutkal. It is special here that Thirumal, who has the right to rule as the deity in the Mullai land, worships Shiva as his Lord. Thus, even though various deities are mentioned as the deities of the lands, Sekkizhar Peruman establishes here, through inference (karuthal alavai), that Lord Shiva is the one who establishes those deities in their respective places and rules as the deity of those deities, and that He is the Supreme God. Although such a Lord Shiva is omnipresent, He resides with excellent manifestation in Thirumullaivayil. Since the Lord manifested under a Mullai creeper, it came to be known as Mullai Vayil. There is a Mullai Vayil near Sirkazhi in Chola Nadu. The Thirumullaivayil mentioned here is located in Thondai Nadu, near Chennai. Therefore, this Thirumullaivayil in Thondai Nadu is called Vadathirumullaivayil (North Thirumullaivayil). The Mullai Vayil near Sirkazhi in Chola Nadu is called Thenthirumullaivayil (South Thirumullaivayil). At this place, once upon a time, when Thondaiman was riding on an elephant, Mullai creepers entangled the elephant's legs, preventing it from moving forward. When he cut those Mullai creepers with his sword, it struck the sacred body of the Lord hidden in the Mullai forest, and the Lord appeared. It is noteworthy that the mark of this incident is still present there. This Vadathirumullaivayil temple is located near Ambattur, near Chennai. In Vadathirumullaivayil temple, the Lord's names are Nirmalamaneeswarar, Pasupatheeswarar, Masilamaneeswarar. The Goddess's names are Latha Mathyambal, Kodiyidai Nayaki, Kodiyidai Ammai. The Sthala Viruksham (sacred tree) is the Mullai creeper, also called Madhavi. The Theertham (sacred water) is Kalyana Theertham, Subramanya Theertham. This Sthalam was known as Rathnapuram in Kritha Yuga, Vilvavanam in Thretha Yuga, Shanbagavanam in Dwapara Yuga, and now shines as Mullaivanam in Kali Yuga. A king named Thondaiman ruled from Kanchipuram. He undertook a Digvijayam (conquest of directions). Two Asuras named Onan and Kanthan ruled from Pulal fort. They ruled with iron pillars, bronze doors, coral pillars, and Bairava worship. These Onan and Kanthan were the ones who consecrated and worshipped a Shiva Lingam in Kanchi and received blessings. That temple is called Onakanthan Thali. Thondaiman came to see them. On his way, while staying and sleeping in a village called Kolambedu, he heard the sound of a bronze bell. Knowing that there must be a Shiva temple there, the king came on an elephant the next morning. Seeing him coming alone, a minor king of the Asuras came with his army and began to oppose Thondaiman. Thondaiman, who had come alone, turned back to bring his army for battle. As he was returning through this Mullai bush, Mullai creepers entangled the elephant's legs. The king, still on the elephant, cut the Mullai creepers with his sword, and blood gushed out. The astonished king dismounted and saw a Shiva Lingam there. The king, regretting his mistake, tried to kill himself with the sword. At that time, the Lord appeared and blessed him, saying, "O King, even if you cut, it is not a fault. I am Masilla Mani (flawless gem), do not grieve. I will send Nandi as your companion. You will be victorious." Since the Lord said, "I am Masilla Mani," He is known as Masilamaneeswarar here. Nandi here is facing east. Knowing that Thondaiman was coming with Nandi Peruman, Onan and Kanthan surrendered. The iron pillars that the king brought from them are still in front of the Lord's shrine today. It is said that the bronze door and coral pillars were placed in Thiruvottriyur, but they were washed away by floods over time. This is the history of the temple. The temple built by Thondaiman here has a Gajaprushta (elephant-back) vimana structure. Prasanna Vinayagar is the Sthala Vinayagar. The Moolavar (presiding deity) is a wonderful Swayambhu Murthy (self-manifested idol), a tall Lingam. The upper part is cut. Sandal paste is always applied. Therefore, only the Avudaiyar (pedestal) receives abhishekam. Once a year, on Chithirai Sadayam, the sandal paste is removed and reapplied.

The Lord remains covered with sandal paste until Chithirai Sadayam. Hot water abhishekam is performed for the Lord, and more sandal paste is applied during abhishekam times, but it is not removed. Therefore, the Lord always has a sandal paste covering. In front of the Lord, outside, a Rasalingam (Lingam made of mercury and silver) has been consecrated. This was consecrated later. This place also has a significant Vaishnavi Devi temple and a Pachaiyamman temple, which is a prayer temple. There is a traditional story about this temple. It has been passed down through generations. It is said that the three idols - Thiruvudai Nayaki Ammai residing in Melur near Minjur on the Chennai-Ponneri road, Vadivudai Ammai residing in Thiruvottriyur, Chennai, and Kodiyidai Ammai here in Vadathirumullaivayil - were all made by the same sculptor. It is also said that on days when Friday and full moon coincide, visiting these three Ambikas sequentially on the same day, in the morning, noon, and evening (Thiruvudai Nayaki in the morning, Vadivudai Nayaki at noon, and Kodiyidai Nayaki in the evening), is considered highly auspicious. The Thirumullaivayil Puranam for this place was composed by Shanmugam Pillai. Thirumullaivayil Andhadhi was composed by Sivagnana Munivar. The books Vadathirumullaivayil Sthothirappa Manjari, Kodiyidai Ammai Irattai Manimalai, Kodiyidai Amman Panjarathinam, Thirumullaivayil Kodiyidai Nayaki Andhadhi, and Vadathirumullaivayil Kodiyidai Ammai Pillaithamil are also available. Now, Sekkizhar Peruman sings about Thiruooral, the next holy place in the Mullai region of Thondai Nadu. Sekkizhar's song describes Thiruooral, also known as Kaligai Maanagar, as a great treasure of the Mullai land of Thondai Nadu. It states that for Lord Shiva, whose sacred body is adorned with sacred ash and whose crescent moon shines on His sacred head, the unique Ganga comes to this world to provide water for His sacred bath, and the water that oozes out from this place, adorned with bright flowers, is given by this Kaligai Maanagar. This place, referred to as Kaligai Maanagar by Sekkizhar Peruman, is Thiruooral, and is currently known as Thakkolam. It is the 12th Sthalam of Thondai Nadu. It is a Sthalam sung by Thirugnanasambandha Peruman. It is the place where Samvarthana Munivar, the younger sage of Surar Guru, performed puja. In the Thiruooral, Thakkolam temple, the Lord's names are Jalanatheeswarar, Umapatheeswarar. The Goddess's names are Giriraja Kannikai, Moganavalli. The Sthala Viruksham here is Nelli, Val Milagu. Nandi Theertham, Parvathi Theertham are the sacred waters. Those who worshipped here include Thirugnanasambandha Peruman, Appar Swamigal, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, Thirumoolar, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, Pattinathu Pillaiyar, Nambiandar Nambigal, Sekkizhar, etc. It is said that this is the place where Daksha's head was severed. Daksha's lament, "Daksha Olai," became "Thakkan Olai" in Tamil and then "Thakkolam." As if to prove this, a Veerapathirar temple is located near the chariot stand, like a fort. The Moolavar (presiding deity) is a Shiva Lingam, a Prithvi Lingam (earth Lingam), a Theenda Thirumeni (untouchable idol). Only the Avudaiyar (pedestal) receives abhishekam. It is an ancient idol. Among the Goshta Murthams (deities in the niches), Dakshinamurthy is seen in a rare posture, with his right leg hanging down and his left leg bent. Siddhantha Sarabam Ashtavathanam Poovai Kalyanasundara Mudaliyar has composed the Sthala Puranam for this place. The Jala Kandeswarar and Gangadhareswarar temples on the banks of the nearby Kallaru are very ancient. To its west is the Sathya Ganga Theertham, and to its east, water once flowed from the Nandi entrance. It is not there now. It is said that the water flowing from the Nandi entrance would circulate around the Shiva Lingam, come out, and then flow from another Nandi entrance, filling the tank, and then flowing into the river. It is a place worshipped by Kamadhenu. Ambal is standing, facing north. It is believed that once there was a flood here, and as a sign that Parvathi Devi embraced and protected Shiva here, there is a depression in the Lingam, and beyond that, one can still see horizontal sand lines on the lower part of the Lingam, as if eroded by the flood. It is believed that Parvathi Devi embraced this Lingam. Therefore, the Sivacharyas (priests) performing puja here do not touch the Lingam and perform abhishekam only to the Avudaiyar. Hence, the Lord here is a Theenda Thirumeni (untouchable idol). This is a great special feature of this place. Besides this, there is another wonder here. During the Uttarayana period, the Lingam appears reddish. At that time, good rain falls, and crops flourish. During the Dakshinayana period, the same Lingam turns white. At that time, the land dries up. Kamadhenu, Indra, Chandra, Yama, Thirumal, Pandavas, Saptha Kanniyargal, Uthathi Munivar, Theerthakad Munivar have worshipped the Lord of this place. Thirunavukkarasu Peruman and Sundaramoorthy Swamigal have sung about this place as Thiruooral. In this village, there are seven Shiva temples, seven Vinayagar temples, and seven village deity temples. Since the Periyaru (big river) flowing here takes a small form and provides oozing water, this Thakkolam is named Thiruooral. Thus, Sekkizhar Peruman sings about the two Shiva Sthalams, Vadathirumullaivayil and Thiruooral, in the Mullai region of Thondai Nadu. We will continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 07 — Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 215, Mummaiyal Ulaganda Sarukkam, Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar Puranam. Sekkizhar Peruman begins to sing about the Marutha land, the third land region of Thondai Nadu. In the Marutha land, many rivers flow with abundant waves. The banks of the fields, where large lotus flowers bloom, are filled with the sounds of birds. Small water bodies within the village are called vaavis (ponds) and kulams (tanks), while those outside the village, being larger, are called large lakes. Here in the Marutha land, a river called Paliyaru flows. It is named Paliyaru because it flows like a surging sacred water, resembling milk. The milk showered from Kamadhenu's udder flows as this sacred water. This river, originating from Nandi Hills, brings pearls, sandalwood, agarwood, and other gems, filling the lotus ponds. This is now called Palaru. Once upon a time, Vasishtar performed penance on Nandi Hills. Kamadhenu, serving in his ashram, was attracted to Lord Shiva's Rishabha Devar. The sage, realizing this, threw a darbha grass, and it transformed into a young calf. Seeing this, Kamadhenu's heart melted with love, and she showered milk on the mountain, which flowed as sacred water and became known as Palaru. This Palaru is the largest river flowing in North Tamil Nadu. When a child touches its mother's breast and puts it to its mouth, the mother's milk flows, satisfying the child's hunger. At other times, the milk is hidden. As soon as the child touches, the milk flows. Similarly, here in Thondai Nadu, when the farmers, known as Mallars, knead the soil with their hands to build embankments, the Palaru flows, releasing its water to help them in their field work. When not needed, the Palaru conceals its water. Other rivers always have water, without distinguishing between times of need and no need. But the Palaru, like a mother's breast, flows with water when needed by the farmers, just as milk flows for a child when it is needed. River water is used for irrigation in two ways. The first type of irrigation is when river water flows directly into the fields. The second type is when river water enters tanks, is stored, and then flows into the fields through sluices and canals. Thus, irrigation facilities are of these two types. In Thondai Nadu, the Palaru directly benefits the fields. Other rivers flowing there, unlike the Palaru, do not go directly into the fields but fill tanks, and from there, through sluices and canals, provide benefit to the fields. The Palaru, like a generous donor who gives directly and happily, gives its resources to others and benefits them. Other rivers are like donors who give through others, as desired by them. In this Marutha land of Thondai Nadu, where the Palaru flows, farmers yoke buffaloes and churn the fields with many plows, making them muddy. They scatter and sow the white sprouts of red paddy, transplanting them. There are also those who pluck the seedlings and those who plant them. Thus, various agricultural activities take place there. Therefore, a great uproar is heard there. Here, Sekkizhar Peruman describes the actions of three types of fish – Vaalai fish, Sel fish, and Varaal fish – in the agricultural areas, as if they are causing hindrance. Vaalai fish obstruct the water flowing in the canals, blocking it. Many Sel fish gather,

Thus, the low-lying fields become elevated like embankments. Varaal fish enter the sluices and block the water flow. In the water-rich Marutha region, there are large fields with boundaries. There, in the sprouted red lotus flowers, there are conches that have conceived and are sleeping, tired. The entire area is illuminated with a mature green glow. Here, red paddy crops grow tall. Beside them are tall sugarcane plants. Beside the sugarcane plants are areca nut trees. Beside the areca nut trees are coconut trees with long bunches. There are banana trees with green fruits. There are long groves with abundant jackfruit and mango trees. Long fences surround these groves. Here, mango fruits with long stalks hang from the branches of the mango trees. Since it is called "theengani" (sweet fruit), the fruit is sweet. But the unripe fruit is sour. These are cited as examples for Thirodhana Shakti (concealing power) and Arul Shakti (graceful power), respectively. That is, the mango fruit is compared to Arul Shakti, and the unripe mango to Thirodhana Shakti in Gnana Shastras. The paddy, sugarcane, areca nut, coconut, banana, jackfruit, and mango mentioned here are the karupporutkal (elements) of the Marutha land. They are also food items. All the villages here are ancient villages. Villages surrounded by cool, large fields. There are houses with paddy granaries. Great families, who welcome and entertain guests with dignity, live their household lives here. The streets there are grand streets with mansions. In this Marutha land, there are prosperous villages where Thirumaraiyavargal (Vedic Brahmins) live. Therefore, the light of reciting the ancient four Vedas and other glorious arts is abundant here. Thus, Sekkizhar Peruman sings about Thiruvallam, Thirumarpedru, and Thiruppasur as the holy places of the Marutha land in Thondai Nadu. Thiruvallam is the tenth holy place in Thondai Nadu. It is located on the banks of the Palaru. This place is called Thiruvallam and Thiruvalam. Here, the Lord's names are Vilvanatheeswarar, Vallanathar. The Goddess's names are Thanumathyambal, Vallambikai. The Sthala Viruksham is Vilvam, and the Theertham is Gowri Theertham. It is a Sthalam sung by Thirugnanasambandar. It is now called Thiruvalam. The Niva river flows through the village. The temple is located on the bank of the river. Since the Lord called the river, "Nee Vaa" (You come) for sacred water, and the river came running and flowed nearby, it was named Niva river. Today, it is known as Ponniyaru. Inside the temple, there are Gowri Theertham and a sacred well. Once upon a time, this area was a Vilva forest. Since Vishnu worshipped Lord Shiva here, Vishnu's footprints are in front of the flagstaff on the Padma Peedam. The Nandi behind the flagstaff and the Nandi in the Moolavar shrine are not facing the Lord but are facing east. Near this Sthalam, there is a hill called Kanjanagiri. It is now called Kanjanagiri. An Asura named Kanjan lived on this hill. In ancient times, sacred water was brought from this hill for the Lord. Kanjan obstructed this. When the devotees complained to Lord Shiva, Lord Shiva went with Nandi Emperuman, fought with Kanjan, and destroyed him. When he was destroyed, the place where the Asura's forehead fell is now called Lalapettai, the place where his head fell is now called Seekarajapuram, the place where his right leg was severed and fell is called Vadakal, the place where his left leg was severed and fell is called Thenkal, the place where his wrist fell is called Maniyampattu, and the place where his chest fell is called Kugaiyanallur. These villages are all located around Thiruvallam.

This event is why Nandi is seated facing east, guarding. In Kanjanagiri, wherever the Asura's blood fell, Shiva Lingams were formed by the grace of the Lord to sanctify the place. Even today, countless Shiva Lingams can be seen directly on the banks of the pond on this hill. Countless Shiva Lingams can also be found by digging. The Niva river flows from north to south and joins the Palaru. In inscriptions, the name of this place is mentioned as Theekalivallam. The sacred vessel of Sanaka Munivar, who worshipped here, is consecrated directly outside the Lord's shrine. The Moolavar (presiding deity) is a Shiva Lingam, a Swayambhu Murthy (self-manifested idol), with a square peedam (pedestal). To the left, in a depression, is the Pathaleswarar shrine. The tradition of the Lord granting liberation to Kanjan is still celebrated as a festival. For this, on the tenth day after Pongal in the month of Thai, the Lord departs from here and proceeds to Kanjanagiri. It is said that on Chithra Pournami (full moon) on this Kanjanagiri hill, if one looks from the banks of the pond, a light appears and then disappears. The Sthala Puranam of Sadayeesar Valla is sung by Sivananda Murthy Swamigal. This Sthalam, called Thiruvallam or Thiruvalam, is located in Tamil Nadu, near Katpadi, on the road from Arcot to Katpadi via Ranipet. Here, the Sthala Vinayagar is called Kanivangiya Pillaiyar (the fruit-receiving Ganesha). This place is called Thiruvalam because Vinayagar circled the Lord, saying, "Ammaiyappar (Mother and Father) are the world." Accordingly, Vinayagar is called Kanivangiya Pillaiyar, and the Lord blesses with a mango in His trunk. At Kanjan's request, Vilvanatheeswarar, on the third day after Thai Pongal, visits the temples built at the eight places where Kanjan's body parts fell and performs the ritual of granting salvation to Kanjan. Sekkizhar Peruman sings another story in his poem. A Brahmin, who knew he had only daughters and no son, performed penance to Lord Shiva, seeking a son. Lord Shiva appeared and said, "You have no fortune to have a son, only daughters." Then, the Brahmin requested, "If so, please bless that these daughters be born to serve you." He received that boon and attained greatness. Sekkizhar Peruman sings this story here. Sekkizhar Peruman sings that this Thiruvallam is a place filled with Brahmins and people who adorn themselves with sacred ash and Rudraksha, which are Shiva's instruments. Now, Sekkizhar Peruman sings about Thirumarpedru, the next Sthalam in the Marutha land of Thondai Nadu. This Sthalam is called Thirumarpedru because Thirumal worshipped here and received the Chakra (disc). This Thirumarpedru is the eleventh Sthalam of Thondai Nadu. In this Sthalam, called Thirumarpedru or Thirumarpur, the Lord's names are Manikanadeeswarar, Dhayanidheeswarar, Pravaleeswarar, Pavalamalaiyar, Vattam Thavirthaar, Mal Vanangeesar. The Goddess's name is Anjanakshi Karunambikai. The Sthala Viruksham is Vilvam, and the Theerthams are Chakra Theertham, Palaru. The history of Thiruveezhimizhalai is also told for this place. Thirumal worshipped daily with a thousand lotus flowers. One day, when one flower was missing, he plucked his own eye and offered it at the Lord's feet. The compassionate Lord, in the form of mercy, bestowed the name Padmakshan upon Thirumal and blessed this place to be known as Thirumarpedru. Thus, Thirumal worshipped and regained his Sudarshana Chakra, which is the history. To confirm this, an Utsava Murthy (processional idol) of Thirumal can be seen in this temple, standing with a lotus flower in one hand and an eye in the other. This Sthalam is sung by Thirugnanasambandha Peruman, Appar Swamigal, Pattinathu Pillaiyar, and Sekkizhar Peruman. The Moolavar (presiding deity) is a Shiva Lingam, a Theenda Thirumeni (untouchable idol). Abhishekam is performed only by covering it with a cup. Here, Vishnu is seen with folded hands, facing the Moolavar. Durga Devi, with eight arms, holding a conch and chakra, is beautifully displayed here. In front of the Moolavar,

There is a tradition of offering Sadari and Theertham to Mahavishnu after the Deeparadhanai (lamp offering) is completed. This temple is mentioned in Kanchi Puranam. Five daily pujas are performed. It is located near Chennai and Kanchipuram in Tamil Nadu. The next holy place in the Marutha land of Thondai Nadu that Sekkizhar Peruman sings about is Thiruppasur. Thiruppasur is the 16th Sthalam of Thondai Nadu. In Thiruppasur temple, the Lord's names are Pasurnathar, Vaseeswarar, Pasupatheeswarar. The Goddess's names are Pasupathi Nayaki, Moganambal. The Sthala Viruksham is bamboo, and the Theerthams are Soma Theertham, Mangala Theertham, Chola Theertham. Thirugnanasambandha Peruman, Appar Swamigal, Sundarar, Paranadeva Nayanar, Pattinathu Pillaiyar, Sekkizhar have sung hymns. Pasu means bamboo. The Lord resides under this bamboo tree. Hence, it is called Pasur. When the Chola king was clearing the forest and making it a country, he cut a bamboo with a Vasu (bamboo-cutting tool), and red blood gushed out from a bush. Seeing a Shiva Lingam there, the Chola king regretted his mistake and built a large temple as atonement. The Jains, on behalf of the Kurumbar king, sent a snake against Karikala Cholan. The Lord appeared, stopped the snake, and took it under His protection. Appar Swamigal mentions this in the Thandagam related to this holy place. Here, during Uchi Kalam (midday), the arts from other Sthalams gather here, so Uchi Kala Darshan is considered special. The Sthala Puranam written by Poovai Kalyanasundara Mudaliyar is named Pasur Puranam. It is located north of Thiruvallur in Tamil Nadu. Thus, Sekkizhar Peruman, who came to sing about the Marutha wealth of Thondai Nadu, sings about the glories of the Shiva holy places located in the Marutha land of Thondai Nadu, namely Thiruvallam, Thirumarpedru, and Thiruppasur. We will continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 08 — Tiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 216, Mummaiyal Ulaganda Sarukkam, Thiruk Kurippu Tonda Nayanar Puranam. Sekkizhar Peruman continues to sing about the riches of Thondai Nadu. Next, he sings about the Neythal land. Neythal is the sea and its surrounding areas. Neythal land is located next to Marutha land. The seashore is a place with white sands. In some places, there are black sands on the seashore, but mostly, white sands are found on the seashore. Sand is very pure. Therefore, it is prescribed in Sivagamas that white sand should be spread on the altars in yagnas and fire should be lit on it. It is said that sea sand is one of the five types of soil placed in the water of the sacred pots consecrated and worshipped for peace and other purposes. The five types of soil are called Panchamirthikai in the Agamas. Thus, the coastal area of Thondai Nadu, the Neythal land, is characterized by white sand.
